The Letters on the Table","volume":"婚后外传：旅馆里藏起来的礼物","anchor":"chapter-0221","paragraphs":["\"Are they giving me a title?\" Mara asked.","Cassian looked genuinely startled. \"No.\"","\"There is a governor beside a stage and a sign saying heir.\"","\"There is also a sign saying heretic.\"","\"My chances are improving.\"","He laughed, then understood why she had not. On the eve of their old lives changing, other people had once tried to tell Mara who she must become. A pretty room could still carry that echo. He drew her into his arms without trying to laugh it away. The music beyond the door was soothing enough to let them stay quiet for a moment.","In response to her question, the retired magistrate emerged carrying a cardboard crown. She was not there to initiate a legal ceremony. The cardboard crown was a symbolic prop. She had agreed to play a fictional judge in the lodge's short opening scene. The governor and mayor had two lines each. Its heroine was a stubborn maid who refused to let an incompetent heir sell the house. The landowner played herself badly, according to the logician, who had been promoted to an unwilling juror. A liberal historian had written the piece, then asked her Marxist friend to scrutinise the jokes.","\"It is time-limited,\" the magistrate assured Mara. \"Twelve minutes, with luck.\"","\"I regard that as a mercy,\" Cassian said.","The costume maid had left her tape-measure on the chair. The educator fetched her foster son, the freshman who had dropped Mara's coat. The kid had been a foster child long enough to distrust a room calling itself family after one conversation. Nobody asked him to do that. He could join the scene, eat supper and decide what he thought afterward. The lodge's gatekeeper would keep his room key safe while he helped with the curtain.","Mara still did not understand the gifts. The gatherer of the family letters was also a careful filer. She brought the first letter. Its giver was a wage earner whose repaired clock had belonged to her mother. She had written a story about hearing it strike again. A genealogist had checked the names in the family note with her, not rewritten her memory. A foreigner staying at the lodge had translated a second letter, asking before changing its tone. The genealogist asked the family how its ethnic background should be recorded in the chart. The small corrections helped, but they did not make the letters anonymous official records.","Madame Roux joined them. The entrepreneur had wanted a quiet celebration of the people whose work kept the house open. Being a millionaire did not require her to finance a ceremony about herself. The one-shot roof grant had paid for one repair. A steady earner of a guesthouse needed many ordinary hands afterward. Her work was the embodiment of a practical hope. She wanted to commend them and had invited Mara to see the objects she had helped return to use.","The manicurist gave up trying to organise everyone and went to help fry the supper potatoes. The Hispanic cook protested that he could manage; his gay husband stole one from the first tray and burned his fingers. The entertainer yelped in comic outrage, making the kitchen laugh. Presently, the first tray of potatoes arrived. A slim young lad in jeans carried plates while a foster parent checked whether he had remembered to eat. A warm room could foster work without making everyone speak the same way.","Ivo and the former miner were testing the chair lift with its authorised operator. The inventor did not despise a user for disliking a screen. The miner was willing to use the lift. He could refresh his view without agreeing about every device. The Luddite did not pretend his objections were all technically informed. The innovator wanted to know what made the controls confusing. The logician’s analytic skill was now devoted to the remaining cake. The humanist professor thought the conversation itself worth observing; the logician thought she should stop blocking the test route. Their combined wisdom was beginning to get in the way.","\"Will the forecaster close that door?\" Rowan called. The meteorologist did so, then corrected his deliberately muddled introduction. Her peer-reviewed weather work had nothing to do with making a village snowstorm sound romantic. The local dweller knew where the path froze first. An uninformed guest was better off listening than trusting an elegant shoe.","The impressionist painter gave Mara the second view of the garden. It did not dwarf the old one. Its purpose was to show what patient work had changed. A former fowler stood beside it, remembering the old hedge. The hunter had helped make the new planting environmentally useful to nesting birds. An article on undertreatment in an entirely different setting lay on Noor's table; Mara helped move it back into the doctor's private folder before anyone treated every page as public reading.","The lawmaker Leda had come to dance, she told a puzzled layman. Her work as a legislator would resume tomorrow. Tonight she would act as an unreliable herald and let a small child become prize-giver who understood cake. The child regarded each winner as a friend and each loser as a future contender. An exhausted matchmaker declared this a more workable system than her own seating chart.","The caretaker returned to hack the fallen branch into sections for removal. A volunteer explained that the literacy class welcomed both literate and illiterate adults in different roles. Its initiator wanted people to help one another. Mara found her kin enjoying the song and returned to Cassian. \"Your secrecy began to work against a good idea. You should have said.\"","\"I thought it would be a pleasant surprise.\"","\"It is. I am catching up.\"","He kissed her temple. She took his hand willingly. His explanation had been lame, but the kindness was real. The person she had thought a foe was simply a lover with a badly explained plan. She could live with that. The little heretic sign turned out to be Rowan's own character, a fool who thought he could improve the family's old stove by shouting at it. Rowan, playing the family heretic, informed him that the house belonged to the people who had patched its roof. The heroine answered that this was not how ownership worked, then ordered both men to move the dripping umbrella. The maid who played her had the audience on her side before the second line.","Mara watched from the front beside Elena and Cassian. On the eve of the lodge's opening, she had imagined a symbolic burden waiting beneath the ribbons. Now she was laughing at two people trying to bow without hitting the scenery. The house's human story was the embodiment of something she trusted: a thing could be loved through use, argument and repair. A one-shot evening could not contain every part of that, but it could offer a beginning.","The retired magistrate wore her fictional robes over jeans. The governor had forgotten a line, the mayor supplied it too early and the juror played by the logician demanded evidence that the stove had been offended. The guesthouse was an earner, but she had not chosen its people from a list of profitable clients. Every dweller tonight had contributed something different to its warmth.","The city dweller Mara had become was surprisingly at home in the lodge. The manicurist danced with the retired miner. A wage earner had written about a restored table; the landowner had described a damaged garden wall. Neither account needed a more important voice to make it matter. Mara was the receiver tonight, and found that role harder than giving. The notes would work against her habit of believing every successful repair vanished once its owner went home.","The picture from the fowler stood beside the impressionist's new view. The doctor would not use a party to discuss undertreatment, diagnoses or anything else the room had not been invited to hear. A soothing cup of tea and a quiet room suited this moment. Her daughter, playing a prophet in the afternoon show, announced the theft through a microphone. A policeman laughed, a policewoman confiscated the microphone and Cassian offered to pay for the pie. Mara decided they had found the right village.","Their host was a retired pilot who now ran a community theatre. Its powerhouse was his wife, a musician who could stop an argument by striking one note. She had invited a novelist and a poet to write a comic play about a rogue realtor. The realtor's fictional rival was a peasant farmer determined to remain a renter rather than sell his land. Mara was to play the farmer. Cassian had been cast, with regrettable accuracy, as a wealthy rake.","The cast included a real postman, a seasonal fruit picker and a retired prosecutor. The plaintiff in their imaginary dispute was a missionary's offspring, now a secular teacher. An actual MP had accepted a smaller part, while a visiting senator agreed to sit in the audience. The president warned them that political speeches would cost extra.","The ticket screener, a cheerful playgoer, wanted to work out whether an official guest counted as a payer. The president declined to interpret the tax code at the gate. Everyone would contribute what they could. Her visiting peer accepted the answer and bought a second ticket, apparently in response to the promise of pie.","Mara helped decorate the courtyard. A Polynesian navigator visiting the local college showed children how to orient a model boat. A Russian postgraduate adjusted its tiny piston with a programmer. Its rudder's pivot kept sticking. A ranger offered to equip them with better tools, and a boy called for his mom to see the repair. His little sister preferred mommy and wanted the boat painted pink.","A truth seeker in the student paper asked regarding the timescale for lunch. His father, universally called Pop, advised patience. Cassian admitted to being an omnivore, though he would draw a line at another wooden-looking cake. His inadequate restraint regarding food amused Mara. She could not undervalue the opportunity for teasing.","The pilot pressed mute on a speaker. A recorded World Cup tournament had been playing since breakfast. Its sound competed with the theatre's rehearsal and a religious choir in the next garden. A nonconformist minister and a republican bookseller were trying to sort out the cables. Neither wished to sear the grass with an overheated extension lead.","A recruitment stall blocked the stage steps. The salesperson wanted to interview everyone; the recruiter beside her seemed to regard each passer-by as a candidate. Mara asked them to move. A sensitive orphan from the youth theatre had begun worrying that the audition queue would penalize him for having no costume. His friend, a refugee student, lent him a jacket. Their joint effort was better than the procedural confusion at the desk.","Inside, a small archive display told the village's less cheerful history. Its old missionary leaflet used the term “Negro” for Black people. A curator's note identified the word as outdated and offensive in present use. An adjacent map labelled a room “Oriental,” another inherited category the museum now explained rather than applied to people. The archive did not ask visitors to repeat either term as a modern description.","An old settler wrote the map's captions. The museum preserved his account alongside responses from the people it misrepresented. Mara was worried about the display being commercially reused without that context. The curator promised it would not. No one should turn a destructive historical category into a decorative modern slogan.","A purist wanted every old label left without explanation. The museum's queer volunteer disagreed. Preservation should not symbolize approval. A religious visitor supported him. A puritan in the play might oppose every change; actual viewers could understand a note. Mara watched a pupil read the explanation, then ask a better question about who had written the leaflet.","Outside, the programme was in trouble. The sound setter had gone home, leaving the violinist's microphone dead. A dog sitter brought a cable, a seed saver brought tape and the bookseller brought a ladder. The theatre's scholastic reputation could not repair a plug. The pilot examined the wiring analytically while Cassian steadied the ladder himself.","The ladder began to pierce the soft turf. Cassian moved it to a firmer board. The first-aid volunteer checked the oxygen capacity of an emergency cylinder before returning it to its case. For a fearful parent nearby, those ordinary preparations were reassuring. They were not urgent, but they would be useful if something went wrong.","The novelist wanted to press forward on rehearsal. The poet wanted lunch. A populist campaigner tried to turn both desires into a speech and received no invitation to continue. “Sir,” the president told him, “the onward march of democracy can wait until after cake.”","The sky darkened. A sudden gust lifted the stage curtain. Cassian caught one corner, Mara another. Behind it, the pie judge's table began to slide down the wet grass. Mara caught the pie; the festival president caught a chair. The policeman and policewoman stopped the table at the gate. Its wheels had not been locked. Nobody needed a prophet to explain the result.","The retired pilot called for everyone to move into the barn. A ranger inspected the route. A passing musician carried a toddler while a postman and fruit picker moved boxes. The programmer and Russian postgraduate rescued the model boat before rain reached its tiny piston. Its pivot had finally been repaired. The youngest pupil protested when someone tried to leave it behind.","Mara saw the refugee student hesitate at the entrance. She thought visitors might need to pay again. The cashier showed her the stamp on her hand; every original payer could return. The orphan actor held the door for her. Their kindness embarrassed the commercially minded salesperson, who had been trying to sell umbrellas at a remarkable price.","A passer-by stopped to help a seasonal worker carry the last bench. The sensitive child had begun to look miserably at the flooded grass. Mara asked her to work out a new entrance for the play. The question brought her attention back. A different route could bring about a better arrival than the original one.","The recruiter offered to help, along with the dog sitter and seed saver. An uninvited downpour had made everyone useful. The World Cup tournament screening moved to a dry wall. The novelist's script travelled in the senator's bag; the poet had protected his verses under a plate. The MP carried a basket with the humble expression of a man discovering what voters expected of him.","The ticket screener warned that the gate might get stuck in the mud. A destructive attempt to force it would make tomorrow harder. Cassian helped prop it safely open before carrying another chair.","The senator agreed to equip the borrowed barn with chairs from his van. A Polynesian visitor carried the folding screen. They fetched a spare curtain from a display about an early settler; nobody would forget to return it. The sound setter found another cable, pressed mute on the faulty speaker and stopped its horrible whine.","A mom called for her daughter. “Mommy, I'm here,” came a furious answer from inside a costume. The little girl had gone to dress as the play's prophet. Mara helped her get rid of the mask before a new prophecy threatened to pierce everyone's hearing. Cassian gave back the stolen hat to its owner, the dog, and found himself applauded for surrender.","The barn offered one problem: an archive case stood beside the proposed stage. It contained the missionary leaflet with “Negro” in its historical text and the old “Oriental” room map. The curator would not let damp coats cover them. A queer volunteer and the nonconformist minister shifted the protective screen together. They had already answered questions about outdated racial terms that afternoon; they would not leave the explanatory note hidden.","The truth seeker from the student paper tried to turn the move into a report. His father, Pop, asked him to carry something first. One had to discover certain facts for oneself, apparently. The youth went miserably after a chair, then found he was enjoying the joint effort more than the interview.","The purist protested again. The visiting peer read the note and supported it. The leaflet's language should not symbolize the village's current welcome. A peasant farmer in a comic play could remain a comic invention; a real settler's account of seizing land required more care. The minister wanted religious and secular audiences alike to see that distinction.","The retired prosecutor helped the novelist move the mock courtroom. The fictional plaintiff was still a missionary's offspring, and the rogue realtor still wanted her land. Their republican bookseller played a judge with no apparent respect for punctuation. The scholar in charge called the result scholastic chaos. The children called it excellent.","Their procedural problem was now who could stand where. The bookseller, a renter in real life, asked regarding the script's extravagant mortgage. The rival actor wanted to press forward on the scene. Mara advised him to wait. Nobody would sear a volunteer's temper merely to save three minutes.","A playgoer worried that the rain would cancel the music. The president refused to penalize anyone for the weather. The revised timescale gave the choir ten minutes and the actors twenty. The powerhouse musician would handle transitions. “Onward,” she said, before a populist interruption could claim the microphone.","Cassian asked whether the farmer might marry the rake in the revised ending. Mara looked him over. “He has lost his property, hat and authority.”","He was worried about the hat's owner, who had begun demanding a biscuit. The child called the dog an omnivore. Cassian thought this undervalued its ambition. 'Sir,' the boy told him, 'you must pay it.' The resulting tribute was commercially disastrous for the biscuit stall but highly profitable for the dog.","“He remains sincere.”","“He can prove it by carrying the next bench.”","He obeyed. A puritan could have found much to criticize in the glance she gave him.
